# AMAZON.COM, INC. — STAGE 1 CLAIM-RECORDS APPENDIX (SECTIONS B–U)

**Dataset:** THE FOUNDER'S PLAYBOOK · **Company:** Amazon.com, Inc. (company_001) · **Stage:** 1
(1993 idea formation → 1995-12-31; inception 1994-07-05) · **Role:** claim-record appendix to
`stage_1.md`, one record per material claim, per `00_METHOD_AND_STYLE.md` §7.

**Provenance of the text.** This file merges the two claim-record parts into one spine in §7 heading
order (B → U): records B–J from `_parts/s1_claims_AJ.md` (288 records) and records K–U from
`_parts/s1_claims_KU.md` (144 records). Nothing was dropped, renumbered for tidiness, or rewritten:
every Date / Source / Source date / Passage / Conf field and every `[src: …]` trace is the specialist's
own string, no class was upgraded, no number was rounded, and `UNKNOWN` stands where it was recorded.
Per-part front matter, the superseded 46-item local conflict key and both part-level merge/coverage
notes are folded into the `## Coverage note` at the foot.

**Conflict spine.** Every `Conflicts:` reference is keyed to the canonical register U.1–U.42
(`_parts/U_CONCORDANCE.md`, stated in full at §U of `stage_1.md`), which absorbed three independent
local numberings. The key printed at the end of `_parts/s1_claims_AJ.md` is NOT that register — its
U.1–U.50 denote different subjects from the canonical ids of the same numbers — and is superseded
here; the `_parts/s1_claims_KU.md` ids U.1–U.13 were adopted verbatim as canonical and are unchanged.
A reference that could not be mapped is printed `UNMAPPED — see coverage note` rather than guessed.

**Corrections applied in place.** `CORRECTIONS.md` is binding and is applied as visible inline markers,
never silently: COR-01 / COR-02 (the original S-1 is acc. 0000891618-97-001309, filed 1997-03-24; acc.
0000891020-97-000839 is S-1/A No. 5 of 1997-05-14 and 0000891020-97-000755 is No. 3; the FY1997
annual report is form 10-K405; the Cadabra subscription for 1,700,000 shares, the 1,700,000-share
passages and the 1994-09-15 "sole stockholder" recital exist in the ORIGINAL only); **COR-12, which
supersedes COR-03 (2026-09-23)** — the count of 11 employees IS filed at 1995-12-31 (S-1/A No. 3/No. 5),
the original S-1's 1996-01-01 phrasing being corroboration of the same population — with COR-11.2 (151 vs
158 is a restatement on an undisclosed basis, and 256 is a different as-of date, 1997-03-31);
COR-13 (the 1995-10-04 press release contains neither nine mailboxes, nor toll-free, fax or e-mail
ordering: nine addresses and the toll-free line are the original S-1's at l.2174 and l.2179–2180, fax is
Knight Ridder Nov 1995 at Medium, and the release supports online ordering plus the published number);
COR-14.1 (§P P61 and its `quantitative.csv` twin cited S-1/A No. 5 for 151, which is original-only);
COR-14.2 (AUDIT 2's "$245,572 parental figure in the filings" is **not adopted** — it appears in no
filing — so COR-09's wording stands); COR-04 / COR-11.6 (the four S-1 "web page pictures" are a printer's
art-direction instruction — no image ever existed — and their 2.5-million-title and 40%-discount
captions are 1997 numbers); COR-06 (Sheff = interview 1999, published 2000; no contemporaneous
founder-state interview exists for Stage 1); COR-10 (the first round IS disclosed — 23 purchasers,
3,021,000 shares, ≈$0.3333, $1,007,000 with subscriptions 1995-12-06 → 1996-05-16, plus $345,525 at
≈$0.1717 — and the "no round total is disclosed" record is retracted); COR-11.1 ($511,000 is S-1
Tier 1 / FACT, never Sheff, never a founder claim); COR-11.3 (HistoryLink 23230 is Tier 2 at best);
COR-11.4 (cite the launch release as "dated 1995-10-04 (indexed 1995-10-03)"); COR-11.5 (the launch
triangle is one conflict); COR-11.7 ("2,300%" verified absent from the original S-1); and COR-09's
do-not-launder list. Retractions stay visible: this appendix is the audit trail, not the clean copy.

**Filing-lineage ruling applied to the `Corroboration:` field (2026-09-24, AUDIT 5 A-B1 / RD-041; method §3).**
Twenty-two records in this appendix carried `Corroboration: 2` where the two documents counted were **states of
one registration statement** — the S-1 original (acc. 0000891618-97-001309, 1997-03-24), S-1/A No. 3, S-1/A No. 5
(acc. 0000891020-97-000839, 1997-05-14), the final prospectus 424B1 — and/or the FY1997 10-K405 restating them.
Same issuer, same counsel, same auditor, one instrument: those records now read
`Corroboration: 1 (same lineage as S0801)`. **No confidence level moves**, because §3 grants High on a primary
document alone, so the double count was never load-bearing for a rating — the defect it corrects is the count
itself and the "sources per claim" tally a later cross-company pass would otherwise inherit. **Two records were
reviewed and deliberately left at 2**, their second corroboration being a genuinely independent origin:
**L05** (the S-1's ordering wording against the *Los Angeles Times* 1996-12-11 rate — filing vs newspaper,
neither derived from the other) and **P13** (the 1995-10-04 company release against the 1997 filing's
warehousing disclosure — two documents of different vintage and purpose, though the release is `restoration
pending` and self-reported, so its independence is bounded by that). Where an amendment supplies content the
original lacks (No. 5's affiliation split of the 23 purchasers; the "11 to 256 employees" anchor at 1995-12-31)
that is a **new disclosure of the same instrument**, cited with its own accession, and is not corroboration of
the original — the rule COR-02 already applies to version differences.

B03 Claim: The signature number is a math error, and a reported book says so on the record — Bezos interpolated a factor of ~2,300 (a 230,000 percent increase) yet repeated "2,300 percent" growth as his founding fact — Date: 1994 — Source: Brad Stone, The Everything Store — Source date: 2013-10-15 — Passage: "Bezos interpolated from this that Web activity overall had gone up that year by a factor of roughly 2,300—a 230,000 percent increase." — Conf: High — Conflicts: U.6 [src: B-83; A-13; A-15]
B04 Claim: The actual source of the growth statistic is identifiable — John Quarterman's monthly newsletter Matrix News, February 1994, which broke out Web growth: bytes up by a factor of 2,057 and packets up by 2,560 between January 1993 and January 1994 — Date: 1994-02 — Source: Brad Stone — Source date: 2013-10-15 — Passage: "One set of numbers in particular in the February 1994 edition of the newsletter was startling." — Conf: Medium-High — Conflicts: U.6 [src: B-84; A-13]

G. SUPPLY / OPERATIONS — CLAIM RECORDS

G01 Claim: Amazon carries minimal inventory and sources product from a network of book distributors and publishers, purchasing a substantial majority of products from Ingram and Baker & Taylor — Date: 1996-12-31 (as described) — Source: Amazon.com Form S-1, Business — Warehousing and Fulfillment — Source date: 1997-05-14 — Passage: "The Company carries minimal inventory and relies to a large extent on rapid fulfillment from major distributors and wholesalers which carry a broad selection of titles." — Conf: High — Conflicts: U.10 [src: E-15]

G03 Claim: Amazon had no long-term contracts or arrangements with ANY vendor guaranteeing merchandise availability, continuation of payment terms, or extension of credit limits, while two vendors supplied a substantial majority of goods — the load-bearing fact for "purchase per order, no buying power"; no distribution agreement is in the exhibit set, so Ingram/B&T terms are not public for Stage 1 — Date: 1996-12-31; 1997-05-14 — Source: Amazon.com Form S-1, Risk Factors — Reliance on Certain Suppliers; S-1/A MD&A Liquidity — Source date: 1997-03-24; 1997-05-14 — Passage: "The Company has no long-term contracts or arrangements with any of its vendors that guarantee the availability of merchandise, the continuation of particular payment terms or the extension of credit limits." — Conf: High — Conflicts: U.40 [src: E-17; C-25; H-13]
G04 Claim: Order-by-order fulfilment from wholesalers was real and fast enough to run a bookstore without inventory: electronically ordered books often shipped within hours — but "often" is not quantified and no fill-rate statistic appears in the S-1; the enabling infrastructure (Ingram's automated ordering) is documented only from the buyer's side — Date: 1996-97 — Source: Amazon.com Form S-1, Business — Warehousing and Fulfillment / "Merchandise"/"Fulfillment" — Source date: 1997-03-24 — Passage: "Under the Company's arrangements with its distributors, electronically ordered books often are shipped by the distributor within hours of receipt of an order from Amazon.com." — Conf: High (S-1) / Medium (independently) — Conflicts: U.39 [mapped 2026-09-23: AJ-local key "U.38" resolved to canonical U.39 "Observed fulfilment speed and damage" on substance — the record disputes whether distributor shipment "within hours" was quantified, not whether the mechanism existed] [src: E-20; C-24]
G05 Claim: Amazon's order-routing logic split orders into (a) titles fillable quickly via electronic interfaces with distributors and (b) a residual routed to an internal "special order group", with dedicated personnel and custom systems for sourcing hard-to-find books — Date: 1997 (describing 1996 state) — Source: Amazon.com Form S-1, Business — Warehousing and Fulfillment — Source date: 1997-05-14 — Passage: "The Company's proprietary software selects the orders that can be filled quickly via electronic interfaces with vendors, and forwards remaining orders to its special order group." — Conf: High — Conflicts: None [src: E-19]

G13 Claim: In the earliest period Amazon held almost no stock: an order triggered a purchase from a distributor, repackaging and shipment, and customers could wait a week or longer; the company kept only several hundred bestsellers on hand, with the rest ordered from distributors (shipped within two days) or from publishers or authors (delivery can take weeks) — Date: 1995-1996 — Source: LA Times (Cast), HistoryLink; corroborated by Seattle Times 1997-01-05 and CSM 1996-09-18 — Source date: 1996-12-11 — Passage: "The company keeps only several hundred bestsellers on hand, says spokeswoman Jennifer Cast. H17 Claim: The adoption gap was demographic from the start: in the 1999 government comparison the lowest- and highest-income/education brackets differed by tens of percentage points in both computer ownership (8.1 vs 76.5 percent) and internet access (2.9 vs 62.0 percent) — Date: 1994 to 1998 — Source: NTIA, Falling Through the Net (Part I: Household Access) — Source date: 1999 — Passage: "8.1% versus 76.5% for computer ownership, and 2.9% versus 62.0% for Internet access." — Conf: High — Conflicts: None [src: I-19]
H18 Claim: The July 1995 NTIA report ("Falling Through the Net: A Survey of the 'Have Nots' in Rural and Urban America") existed and was the in-window government statement of who did and did not have computers and phone/modem access; its full household-access percentages could not be retrieved in text form, so the specific 1994-95 percentages it published are recorded as UNKNOWN — Date: 1995-07 — Source: ERIC ED399126, NTIA record (title, agency, date) — Source date: 1995-07 — Passage: "Falling through the Net: A Survey of the 'Have Nots' in Rural and Urban America … 1995-Jul" — Conf: High (existence) / UNKNOWN (figures) — Conflicts: U.36 [src: I-20]
H19 Claim: The friction of being online in early 1995 was described in the period's own reporting of the user experience: searches took minutes to untangle and the network returned congestion errors, the practical setting in which a consumer would have placed a book order — Date: 1995-02-26 — Source: "Why the Web Won't Be Nirvana," Newsweek (Science section), Clifford Stoll — Source date: 1995-02-26 — Passage: "'Too many connections, try again later.'" — Conf: Medium — Conflicts: None [src: I-60]
H20 Claim: Modem speeds and monthly access prices in 1994-96, and the typical retail price of a home computer in the same window, were not established from an in-window source; trade-press reviews and ISP rate cards were not retrieved, so these are recorded as unquantified rather than importing later figures — Date: 1994 to 1996 — Source: UNKNOWN — Source date: UNKNOWN — Passage: NO_VERBATIM_PASSAGE_RECORDED — Conf: UNKNOWN — Conflicts: None [src: I-62]

H22 Claim: In the same January 1997 release the company sized itself against the incumbent format rather than against the internet: "Amazon.com's catalog is more than six times the size of the largest conventional bookstore." — Date: 1997-01 — Source: "Amazon.com Announces 1996 Bestsellers," Amazon.com press release — Source date: 1997-01-09 — Passage: "Amazon.com's catalog is more than six times the size of the largest conventional bookstore." — Conf: High — Conflicts: U.3 [src: I-42; I-41]
H23 Claim: A later industry profile asserts that mail-order house sales "skyrocketed to $211 billion by 1990," grew "more than 9.9 percent per year" from 1990 to 1996, and were "more than 3 percent of retail sales" — i.e. ordering sight-unseen by post was an established, large-scale American behavior before 1995 — but the $211 billion 1990 figure is stated without basis and is implausibly large against total U.S. retail sales that year, and must not be carried into the stage file without a Census basis — Date: 1990 to 1996 (as reported later) — Source: "SIC 5961 Catalog and Mail-Order Houses," Encyclopedia of American Trade Profiles, The Gale Group Inc. — Source date: 2000-2003 (RETROSPECTIVE SOURCE) — Passage: "mail order sales grew at a rate of more than 9.9 percent per year" — Conf: Low — Conflicts: U.38 [src: I-43]

J05 Claim: A dedicated staff function — "systems administrators and network managers" — monitored and operated the Web site, network operations and transaction-processing systems; operations was separated from development by 1997 — Date: as of 1997-03 — Source: Form S-1, "Business — Technology" — Source date: 1997-03-24 — Passage: "A group of systems administrators and network managers monitor and operate the Company's Web site, network operations and transaction-processing systems." — Conf: High — Conflicts: None [src: F-05]
J06 Claim: Amazon disclosed in its own filing that its transaction-processing system was NOT integrated with its accounting and financial systems, and that financial reporting required "a significant amount of manual effort" — the strongest primary-document evidence that bookkeeping was not automated in Stage 1 — Date: as of 1997-03 (describing the system built through 1996) — Source: Form S-1, "Business — Technology"; Risk Factors — Source date: 1997-03-24 — Passage: "The Company's transaction-processing system is not integrated with the remainder of the Company's accounting and financial systems." — Conf: High — Conflicts: None [src: F-06; E-22]
J07 Claim: Substantially all of Amazon's computer and communications hardware sat at a single leased facility in Seattle, with no redundant systems and no formal disaster recovery plan, as self-disclosed to the SEC — Date: as of 1997-03 — Source: Form S-1, "Risk Factors — Risk of System Failure; Single Site and Order Interface" — Source date: 1997-03-24 — Passage: "The Company does not presently have redundant systems or a formal disaster recovery plan." — Conf: High — Conflicts: U.10 [src: F-07]

N. FOUNDER DECISIONS — CLAIM RECORDS

N01 Claim: Bezos left D.E. Shaw, employment ending June 1994, to found the company, his stated motive being ownership economics rather than insight; he also reports forgoing his 1994 Wall Street bonus, which no source quantifies — Date: 1990-12 to 1994-06 (employment); 1994 (decision) — Source: Amazon.com Form S-1, "Executive Officers and Directors" (T01); Sheff (T06); Stone (T08) — Source date: 1997-03-24; 2000; 2013-10-15 — Passage: "From December 1990 to June 1994, Mr. Bezos was employed by D.E. Shaw & Co., a Wall Street investment firm, becoming Senior Vice President in 1992." — Conf: High on dates; Medium on motive; Low on any bonus amount — Conflicts: Stone's "one of the youngest vice presidents" is a superlative neither filing supports — the word "youngest" appears in neither; the bonus appears in no primary filing. N02 Claim: Bezos says a self-invented heuristic, projecting himself to age 80 to minimise regrets, decided the resignation rather than analysis — Date: 1994 (claimed decision), 1999 (uttered) — Source: Sheff / Playboy interview (T06) — Source date: 2000 (interview conducted 1999) — Passage: "You project yourself to age 80 and look back on your life. You want to minimize the number of regrets." — Conf: High that he consistently used the frame; UNKNOWN that it governed the 1994 choice — Conflicts: Retrievable as early as 1999, so not a late invention, but Stone gives a shared provenance — the couple were then videotaping oral-history interviews with MacKenzie's eighty-year-old grandfather. N03 Claim: He chose books from an explicit shortlist of roughly twenty product categories, on supply-side grounds — commodity goods, only two primary US distributors, and an in-print universe no shelf could carry — Date: 1994 (early-mid) — Source: Stone excerpt; Sheff; Littman/LA Times; Kaphan interview (T08, T06, T07, T12) — Source date: 2013-10-15; 2000; 1997-07-20; 2011-06-14 — Passage: "He made a list of twenty possible product categories, including computer software, office supplies, apparel, and music." — Conf: Medium — Conflicts: Three retellings trace to Bezos and count as ONE source; no 1994-95 document lists the twenty. T. SOURCE / PROVENANCE TABLE — CLAIM RECORDS

One record per distinct source DOCUMENT: title, type, primary or secondary, event date, publication date, URL, tier, confidence, plus a `restoration pending` flag where the document was cited only from the destroyed `_scratch` copy and is not yet re-downloaded to `sources/`. An `independence note` records when a document rests on another already in this register: repeated copying of one story is ONE source, not corroboration.

T01 Claim: Amazon.com, Inc. Form S-1 (original), full text plus exhibits 10.12 subscription, 10.13, 10.15, 10.28-10.31 leases, 2.1 merger; CIK 1018724; SEC File No. 333-23795 — Type: SEC filing; Primary — Event date: 1994-07-05 to 1996 — Publication date: 1997-03-24 — URL: https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1018724/0000891618-97-001309.txt — Tier: 1 — Conf: High — Restoration: ON DISK, `sources/S-1_original_acc-0000891618-97-001309_filed-1997-03-24.txt` and `sources/s1_original_0000891618-97-001309.txt` — Conflicts: Several dossiers mis-cite this as accession 0000891020-97-000839, which is in fact the S-1/A No. 5 of 1997-05-14; the two must be cited separately and their dates never conflated. The S-1's "History of the Enterprise" heading does not exist in this accession; the equivalent narrative sits in Prospectus Summary and MD&A. [src: E-81b; cache corrections]
T02 Claim: Amazon.com, Inc. Form S-1/A Amendment No. 5 (full submission including exhibits); also on disk: S-1/A No. 3 of 1997-05-09 — Type: SEC filing; Primary — Event date: 1994 to May 1997 — Publication date: 1997-05-14 — URL: https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1018724/0000891020-97-000839.txt — Tier: 1 — Conf: High — Restoration: ON DISK, `sources/S-1A-No5_acc-0000891020-97-000839_filed-1997-05-14.txt`, `sources/s1_0000891020-97-000839.txt`, `sources/S-1A-No3_acc-0000891020-97-000755_filed-1997-05-09.txt` — Independence note: **RE-KEYED 2026-09-24 under the method §3 filing-lineage rule (AUDIT 5 A-B1 / RD-041).** T02 is a second **accession of one registration statement**, not a second document: same issuer, same counsel, same auditor, and the amendment is a later draft of the instrument T01 filed. Disclosures appearing in both T01 and T02 (the "Earth's Biggest Bookstore" sentence, ISP connectivity, non-integrated accounting, the nine customer-service e-mail addresses, order-splitting software) therefore rest on **one instrument — `same lineage as S0801`, corroboration 1**, and the earlier wording that they "now rest on two independent documents rather than one" is withdrawn. What the accession distinction *does* carry is **version evidence**: it settles which draft said what, and under COR-02 it is load-bearing in the other direction — "Cadabra, Inc.", the 1,700,000-share subscription and the 1994-09-15 sole-stockholder recital exist in **T01 only** and must never be cited to T02. Two T02-only additions are recorded as new disclosure of the same instrument, not as corroboration of it: the Item 5 affiliation split of the 23 purchasers (l.4665–4668) and the "from December 31, 1995 … 11 to 256 employees" anchor (l.796). The same cap applies to **T03** (FY1997 10-K405), which restates the registration text and is independent of it only for what the filings never carried [src: H; G; F-86; F-87]

U. CONFLICTING EVIDENCE — CLAIM RECORDS

**Register note.** The canonical conflict register is U.1–U.42, fixed by `_parts/U_CONCORDANCE.md` and stated in full at §U of `stage_1.md`. The thirteen records below are the conflict records this appendix carries in its own hand — written as `_parts/s1_claims_KU.md` §U and adopted verbatim as canonical U.1–U.13. U.14–U.42 live in the register, not as duplicate records here, and every `Conflicts:` reference in sections B–T is keyed to the final ids. Where a record below absorbs a superseded local key, the absorption is recorded in the register entry.

One record per contradiction, both sides kept, with a BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION and a RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY. Nothing here is reconciled silently.

U.1 Claim: LAUNCH WINDOW. CLAIM A: the filings say Amazon "commenced offering products for sale on its Web site in July 1995" with "no sales" "through July 1995", while its 4 October 1995 release counted only "its first four weeks of operation". CLAIM B: the canon dates the first sale to 16 July 1995, and one buyer claims 3 April 1995. WHY THEY DIFFER: the day-level date is anniversary memory, SEC language is month-level and accounting-driven, and the release may define "operation" as unrestricted public ordering rather than the first transaction — or "four weeks" may be loose PR arithmetic, since a 16 July opening would make it eleven weeks. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: the S-1 and the 1995 release are Tier 1 and mutually compatible with a July-September opening; the July-16 canon is Tier 2/3 and ten years downstream. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: Amazon could transact in July 1995; the only dated company statement about when outside demand appeared is "first four weeks of operation" as of 4 October 1995; report the launch as "July 1995 per SEC filings, publicly announced 3-4 October 1995" and refuse a day. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: the day is unrecoverable from Tier-1 sources, and an April-1995 invite-only order or a ~May-1995 soft launch would mean the first transaction predates every official dating. CONFIDENCE: High in the interpretation, High in the residual. [src: D-05; D-01; E-02; A-60; A-62]
U.2 Claim: THE FIRST-SALE LEGEND, FIVE VARIANTS. CLAIM A: John Wainwright ordered Hofstadter's "Fluid Concepts and Creative Analogies" on 3 April 1995 for $27.95 after Kaphan emailed him. CLAIM B: the same Atlantic article states that "Amazon itself dates that first book sale to July 1995, when it officially opened its doors to the public". CLAIM C: the first item was "a science textbook" sold 16 July 1995. CLAIM D: Amazon's own 2007 IR timeline (T15) reads "1995 July / Amazon.com Sells First Book". CLAIM E: a prospectus sentence circulated online, "we officially launched our Web site in July 1995 and shipped our first book in August 1995", absent from all five retrieved SEC documents. WHY THEY DIFFER: anniversary compression, conflation of first ORDER with first SHIPMENT, a curated company PR event, and at least one quotation with no document behind it; Sheff separately reports a six-week beta of ~300 real charged friend-and-family orders before opening, which contradicts any single "first sale". EVIDENCE WEIGHT: none is contemporaneous; D is company-authored but 2007; E is unverifiable; A alone is tied to a physical packing slip and the buyer's own order history. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: Hofstadter's book is the best-attested first NON-EMPLOYEE order; "science textbook" is a degraded variant of the same story; "shipped our first book in August 1995" is untraced and unusable; and because real first transactions were probably insider beta purchases, "the first customer" cannot be established from retrieved evidence at all. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: no Amazon document naming a first title was found, and the company never identified one during Stage 1. CONFIDENCE: Medium on A, Low on the rest. [src: D-08; D-09; D-10; D-04; D-12; G-15]
U.3 Claim: CATALOG SIZE. CLAIM A: ">1,000,000 different titles" (Amazon's own release, 1995-10-03), rising to 1.1 million (January 1997). CLAIM B: "more than 2.5 million titles" (S-1, May 1997). WHY THEY DIFFER: not a conflict once dated — roughly eighteen to twenty-one months of licensed-data growth, including an out-of-print service launched only in March 1997. The substantive finding is that in BOTH years the advertised figure outran the orderable figure, only up to about 400,000 titles being distributor-supplied. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: both Tier 1, both the company's own statements, each authoritative for its own date. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: Stage-1 advertised selection is more than one million; 2.5 million is a 1997 number and must never be used for Stage 1; Kaphan's gloss is the operative definition, "we did have a million titles. We didn't have a million books", so the advertised number was a listing claim, not an inventory fact. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: the exact 31 December 1995 catalog count is not disclosed and lay somewhere between 1M and 2.5M. CONFIDENCE: High. [corrected 2026-09-23: the launch release is cited "press release, dated 1995-10-04 (indexed 1995-10-03)"; the one-day gap is a minor-dating item at U.16 — COR-11.4] [src: E-71; E-18; J-21; J-40; G-37; A-55]
U.4 Claim: ORIGINAL NAME, AND "CADAMIA". CLAIM A: the founding legal name was "Cadabra, Inc., a Washington corporation", signature-level and dated 5 July 1994. CLAIM B: the widely copied assertion that the company was first "Cadamia, Inc." WHY THEY DIFFER: B has no documentary antecedent at all — the word "Cadamia" appears nowhere in the 1.44 MB S-1 text searched, nor in any retrieved source. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: A is Exhibit 10.12 under securities-law liability, corroborated by Kaphan 2011 and Stone 2013 and by RDAP showing cadabra.com unregistered until 2003-12-26, i.e. the 1994 name was corporate only and never a web identity; B is unsourced. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: "Cadabra, Inc." is a primary-source fact and "Cadamia" is refuted by the filing itself; separately the DATE of the change to Amazon.com, Inc. is UNKNOWN, bounded by the amazon.com registration of 1994-11-01 and mid-1995 public identity, and Exhibit 10.13's "Amazon.com, Inc. … a Delaware corporation" dated 1995-02-09 must NOT be used to date it because the company was not Delaware until 1996 and the exhibit is a conformed template. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: none as to the name; complete as to the change date, pending the Washington registry record at S09. CONFIDENCE: High on A; High as a negative finding on the date. [src: A-04; A-39; A-44; A-45]
U.5 Claim: US BOOK MARKET SIZE. CLAIM A: "U.S. book sales were estimated to be approximately $26 billion in 1996" (Euromonitor, quoted in Amazon's S-1; worldwide ≈$82 billion). CLAIM B: a narrower consumer-book-retailing basis of roughly $11 billion, on the order of the $10-12 billion the trade used, which is the only scale consistent with the same filings' competitive math — top two retailers under 25 percent of sales, B&N superstores ≈9 percent of the consumer book market. WHY THEY DIFFER: definitional — an international research firm's "book sales" can include educational, professional, religious, serial and non-consumer channels, or measure publisher or wholesale turnover rather than retail receipts, and the S-1 does not define what Euromonitor counts. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: only A carries a named source and a date inside retrieved material; B's underlying trade figure was NOT retrieved, so B cannot be asserted here. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: for Stage-1 purposes treat any dollar market size as UNKNOWN-in-this-dossier and build the case on STRUCTURE — titles in print versus titles stocked versus titles sourceable — which is multiply evidenced. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: without an AAP or Bowker primary, neither the level nor the definition of the 1994 market can be fixed. CONFIDENCE: Medium on A, Low on the reconciliation. [src: C-18; I-24; C-31; C-32]
U.6 Claim: THE "2,300%" TRIGGER STATISTIC. CLAIM A: Bezos read that the Web was growing 2,300 percent a year and that decided it — the earliest retrievable printed version being the LA Times of 1997-07-20, "happened upon a site projecting annual Web growth at an astounding 2,300%", a figure Amazon's IPO filings never mention at all. CLAIM B: Stone reconstructs the underlying charts — John Quarterman's *Matrix News*, February 1994, bytes up 2,057-fold and packets up 2,560-fold from January 1993 to January 1994 — and states that Bezos interpolated a factor of roughly 2,300, "a 230,000 percent increase", so "Amazon began with a math error." WHY THEY DIFFER: A restates a cumulative twelve-month multiple as an annual percentage growth rate and compresses a multi-week research process into a found fact, which is how the founder usually told it. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: B is the only version naming a document, a date and a calculation; A is a founder refrain whose later repetitions are not independent sources; against both, three contemporaneous measurements — Netcraft's 603,367 responding sites in December 1996 and 1,117,259 in June 1997, about 85 percent in six months, IDC's ≈35 million users at end-1996, and the CERN-era server list in the tens — do not support a 2,300-percent-per-year rate. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: record the 2,300 percent as a FOUNDER CLAIM of unstated provenance; the defensible statement is that a 1993-94 Web traffic series exhibiting roughly 2,000-fold annual growth convinced him the medium was inflecting; do NOT carry 2,300 percent into the metrics table as a fact. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: the Quarterman newsletter has not been examined, so whether it printed "2,300%" is UNKNOWN, and no 1994-dated document ties the number to the decision. CONFIDENCE: High on the challenge, Medium on Stone's reconstruction. [src: E-67; G-01; G-02; B-83; B-84; C-15; C-05; K-10]
U.7 Claim: THE 1994 PLANNING DOCUMENT SITS AT RUNG 3. CLAIM A: a titled, page-counted roadmap — "the roadmap", "91 pages", "Future of Computer Stores" — circulated in 1994 and setting out the everything-store plan. CLAIM B: rungs 1 and 2 are empty and the ladder tops out at rung 3, founder description — a 1997 laptop document, a 1999 prose draft written in the car, a 2013 Excel projection — three mutually inconsistent physical descriptions of one artifact from one witness. CLAIM C: the audited S-1 is a hard negative control, never mentioning a planning document, containing neither the word "roadmap" nor the phrase "business plan" in 1.44 MB, stating its founding purpose as online BOOK retailing, and relegating category expansion to a Risk Factor. WHY THEY DIFFER: A has no lineage whatsoever — exact-phrase searches return only Facebook, Instagram and LinkedIn aggregation with zero authoritative hits — and its likeliest mechanism is a genuine but unremarkable circulated startup plan, progressively inflated in the retelling into a named, page-counted prophetic artifact once the outcome made such an artifact valuable. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: B is supported by the artifact's own drift; C is Tier 1 under securities-law liability; A is Tier 5; and repeated copying is one source, Wikipedia's 1994 chronology citing only Stone 2013 while Stone's Chapter 1 neither describes a 91-page plan nor quotes any planning document, Brandt's book being 2011 and unread (T22). BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: a short working plan, probably typed and partly numerical, drafted on the 1994 drive and circulated to family investors who "reviewed the business plan"; its length, title, scope and survival are undocumented. The narrative may state ONLY that Bezos described drafting a plan during the drive and that no copy, text, page count or title has ever surfaced; it may NOT name a roadmap, a 91-page notebook or "Future of Computer Stores", and may not attribute an everything-store conception to 1994 except as a D.E. Shaw conversation recalled in 2013, Bezos's own statement on scope being "at first, all we knew is that we were going to sell books." RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: whether anything longer than a working plan existed, and whether a paper copy survives in private hands, the parents or early investor Nick Hanauer's circle being the plausible custodians. CONFIDENCE: High that rungs 1-2 are empty; Medium that a short plan existed. [src: K-05; K-06; K-11; K-14; K-16; K-21; K-29; K-30; K-32; B-93; G-04]
U.8 Claim: THE FRIENDS-AND-FAMILY ROUND. CLAIM A: "$1.1 million from 22 investors at $50,000 each", often with "for about 20 percent at a $5 million valuation", plus "$150,000 from his parents in 1995" restated as "almost $250,000". CLAIM B: the S-1 records only three named related-party common purchases totalling ≈$295,568, an FY1995 financing inflow of $1.2 million "primarily from the issuance of Common Stock and the exercise of stock options" — audited at exactly $1,272,000 — and NO round total, participant count, valuation or percentage sold. WHY THEY DIFFER: A is memoir and press compressed into a single event, while B is a filing reporting cash received and named insiders with no reason to bundle the rest; the "$250,000" specifically is a 2018 newspaper inference, an inflation template around $245,573 built from LATER share holdings, and "$150,000" is an unreconciled competing figure from a 2013 book relayed by a 2025 essay. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: B for anything numerical; A survives only as an order-of-magnitude corroboration, and the element that survives across independent lineages is the COUNT, 20 to 22, not the AMOUNT. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: approximately $1.2 million of common-equity cash reached Amazon during calendar 1995, of which about $296,000 traces to three named insiders; parental investment of roughly $245.5K across the two 1995 share purchases, ≈$100,020 plus ≈$145,553, is the best-supported reconstruction of the family figure; and the 22-person, $50,000-each, $5 million-valuation packaging is NOT primary-evidenced and should be tagged memoir. Note also that the Kleiner Perkins $8.0 million arrived in June 1996, a year later and a different investor class, so collapsing the two into "the first $1 million raise" is the most common error in this space. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: the identity and count of the residual ≈$0.9 million of purchasers, option-exercise proceeds being commingled with issuance proceeds and inseparable. CONFIDENCE: High on totals, Low on structure. [corrected 2026-09-23: was "NO round total, participant count, valuation or percentage sold" — RETRACTED: the filing discloses 3,021,000 shares to 23 purchasers at ≈$0.3333 = $1,007,000 (subscriptions 1995-12-06 → 1996-05-16, so mostly post-boundary) plus 2,012,772 shares to three founder-related investors at ≈$0.1717 = $345,525; the per-investor split, the roster of 23 and any valuation stay undisclosed, so the legend stays untraced, and $1,272,000 (CY1995 equity cash) versus $1,007,000 reconciles as DERIVED, never averaged — COR-10 / U.8] [corrected 2026-09-23 — COR-14.2: the clause "parental investment of roughly $245.5K across the two 1995 share purchases … is the best-supported reconstruction of the family figure" is **withdrawn and must not be re-imported**. SUBSTITUTION: AUDIT 2 asserted the folk "$250,000 from the parents" equals a filings figure of **$245,572**; a direct search for that string across the original S-1, S-1/A No. 5 and the 10-K405 returns **nothing**, so the equation is unverified and is **not adopted** — the auditor is not exempt from the rule it enforces. COR-09's wording is restored: **$150,000 and "almost $250,000" are UNTRACED, and the $250,000 traces to a 2018 LA Times inference** (Metcalf, via Wikipedia) reverse-engineered from later holdings. What survives is only register arithmetic on two filed purchases (582,528 and 847,716 shares at ≈$0.1717 ≈ $100,020 + $145,553 ≈ $245,573), which is a **named-related-party floor**, is labelled DERIVED, is filed nowhere as an amount, and is **not** identified as the parents' contribution — U.8, §K.5(c), P.2 d23] [corrected 2026-09-24 — verify-3 F-4/F-3 (RD-032 / RD-031), body text above left verbatim: **the clause "RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: the identity and count of the residual ≈$0.9 million of purchasers, option-exercise proceeds being commingled with issuance proceeds and inseparable" is WITHDRAWN in its entirety and must not be followed.** Both halves failed. (i) *Commingling* is refuted by the audited statement of stockholders' equity, which prices 1995 option exercises at **$—** (S-1 (orig.) l.3560–3562): no option cash is inside the residual at all. (ii) *"roughly $0.9 million"* is neither a filed line nor the canonical rendering, which is **≈$976,000 (±$1,000)**. What the residual is: three filed legs — **$5,408 + $150,000 − $50,000 = $105,408** — plus a **balance that is UNKNOWN both as to attribution and as to figure**, because the fourth leg this record's family printed twice ($871,024, then $871,000 = 2,613,000 ÷ 3) rested on the **unfiled** 2,613,000 and is retracted with it; **no third number is substituted** (see the RETRACTED-FIGURE RECORD at `stage_1.md` §U.8, `conflicts.csv` U.8, §P.2 d8a/d25, `quantitative.csv` L35/L99). The `EVIDENCE WEIGHT` line's "A survives only as order-of-magnitude corroboration" is retained; the count that survives across independent lineages is the purchaser count (20 to 22), not the amount — and per the method §3 filing-lineage rule the surviving Tier-1 side is **one instrument**, so the residual's arithmetic rests on a single audited statement, not on two] [src: E-84; E-85; E-26; E-27; E-76; B-40; B-47; B-110; B-122; G-22; G-24]
U.9 Claim: STAGE-1 SALES MAGNITUDE AND BASIS. CLAIM A: weekly first-run sales of $12,000 and $14,000, with variants "$20,000 per week within two months" and "about $1 million in the first 30 days". CLAIM B: audited FY1995 net sales of $511,000. WHY THEY DIFFER: three ways at once — basis, since "returns" is an order-taken measure while the S-1 recognises revenue on SHIPMENT, leaving late-December orders in 1996; period, the weeks being undated launch weeks rather than a run-rate; and metric, net-of-returns versus gross-of-discount. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: B is audited by Ernst & Young while A is a tertiary local-history page with no cited primary; measured against B, 1995 averaged about $21,000 per week across the whole launch year, so the "$20,000 a week" anecdote describes an ordinary ramp rather than a detonation, and the "$1 million in 30 days" variant is flatly inconsistent with the audited annual figure and is classified folklore. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: use $511,000 for calendar 1995 and treat the weekly figures as unfalsifiable colour, noting that $12K plus $14K over two weeks is not arithmetically hostile to $511K over some twenty-six weeks — the mismatch is about basis and dating, not magnitude. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: high, since without an order count or a monthly series no reconciliation is possible, and because quarterly disclosure begins only at Q1 1996 any Stage-1 monthly or weekly curve is by construction non-primary. CONFIDENCE: High on B, Low on A. [src: E-04; E-35; E-77; E-93; A-63; D-12; G-17]
U.10 Claim: PHYSICAL AND HUMAN SCALE AT STAGE END. CLAIM A: "The Company carries minimal inventory", with Bezos in 1999 describing "a basement" and then "a real office with a 400-square-foot warehouse", HistoryLink describing a "200-square-foot warehouse" in SODO, and memoir reporting "four employees". CLAIM B: FY1995 cash flow was reduced by an inventory increase of exactly $17,000, $0 in the 1994 stub, with a further $554,000 build in FY1996; Note 1 sets a lower-of-average-cost-or-market policy; gross equipment was $81,000 with ZERO leasehold improvements; employees numbered 11 at 31 December 1995; and the S-1 discloses a Seattle warehouse through which all sales passed. WHY THEY DIFFER: A is a relative marketing claim, minimal measured against a bookstore's stock, plus two unfenced recollections of a space nobody measured and that was immaterial enough to appear in no filing, while B is absolute accounting. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: A and B draw on the same filing for the inventory point, where B is the harder evidence, and for premises and headcount B is under securities law where A is memory. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: Amazon was a buy-on-order business that nonetheless carried inventory from its first trading year, but so little that the whole FY1995 position was ≈$17,000, about 3.3 percent of net sales, so the "no inventory" myth is unsupported and so is any suggestion that Stage-1 Amazon was inventory-intensive; stage-1 premises square footage is UNKNOWN, what IS evidenced being $0 of leasehold improvements at 31 December 1995, an independent check that the operation had still not built out occupied space; headcount of 4 to 11 is the right order of magnitude for 1995 and "four" is a rounded recollection. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY — CORRECTED (verify-3 F-9 sibling sweep): the $17,000 balance IS lifted from a balance-sheet line (orig. l.3429, inside the audited statements) and is FACT (audited); what the register previously said — that it "is DERIVED from cumulative period movements rather than lifted from a balance-sheet line", with high uncertainty on the number — was wrong in the conservative direction and is struck (D15 CLOSED; §P39, quantitative.csv L70, validation.csv r19/r20, stage_1.md §U.10). What survives as genuine uncertainty is only Note 1's disclosure that "certain prior-year balances have been reclassified", which affects the 1994 comparative, not the 1995 figure; low on the conclusion. CONFIDENCE: High on the audited lines, UNKNOWN on square footage. [corrected 2026-09-23 — COR-12 supersedes COR-03 (2026-09-23): the count is filed at 1995-12-31 in S-1/A No. 3/No. 5, "From December 31, 1995 to March 31, 1997, the Company expanded from 11 to 256 employees", so the downgrade previously layered on this record is WITHDRAWN and its 1995-12-31 basis restored; the original S-1's "from January 1, 1996 to December 31, 1996 … 11 to 151 employees" is independent corroboration of the same population at the 1995/96 turn, not a competing date, and 11 is not styled "full-time" there. 151 (1996-12-31), 158 (10-K405, 1996-12-31) and 256 (1997-03-31) are post-boundary counts on stated dates and are not reconciled to one number. History kept visible: this record was corrected, and that correction was itself corrected — U.17] [src: E-87; E-32; E-15; E-44; E-61; E-77; E-92; G-07; G-09]
U.11 Claim: PRIORITY AND THE ADVERTISED POSITION. CLAIM A: Amazon was the pioneer — "Earth's Biggest Bookstore", the motto "If it's in print, it's in stock", and "first mover advantages and momentum" asserted in its own filing, the LA Times calling it "the world's first electronic bookstore". CLAIM B: the company's own lawyers named incumbents — Book Stacks Unlimited, a CUC International subsidiary whose start is given as 1991 by Wired and 1992 by the Seattle Times, other small entrants, AOL and Microsoft as channel aggregators, Simon & Schuster going direct — the NCSA Mosaic "What's New" listing of 2 August 1995 already shows multiple commercial web book sites, contemporaneous press stated Amazon "isn't the first or necessarily the cheapest online book merchant", and B&N sued on 12 May 1997 to block "Earth's biggest bookstore" as false advertising, arguing Amazon is "a 'book broker' and not a 'bookstore'" whose warehouse stocks a few hundred titles. WHY THEY DIFFER: "first" is usually asserted on chronology while Amazon's claim is about breadth of listed selection and brand, and competitors and reporters counted catalogues differently. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: Tier 1 on both sides, including Amazon's own competition disclosure, and the S-1 itself claims only "first mover", not priority. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: Amazon was not the first web bookstore; its Stage-1 distinctiveness must be argued from operational filings — directory discoverability, tiered discounting, e-mail notification and centralized sourcing over the 350,000-to-400,000 fillable pool — not from priority, and its slogan was under legal attack while the origin narrative was being built. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: Book Stacks' own start year conflicts between two 1996-97 sources, so even the runner-up position is uncertain. CONFIDENCE: High. [src: E-79; E-80; D-69; D-67; G-12; G-14; G-36]
U.12 Claim: COMPANY-SUPPLIED RATE METRICS ARE NOT COMPARABLE. CLAIM A: 60 percent of orders came from repeat customers, Bezos to the WSJ in May 1996, and sales grew 34 to 35 percent per month sustained for 12 to 18 months, in three company-supplied variants between September 1996 and January 1997. CLAIM B: "repeat customers currently account for over 40% of orders" in the March 1997 S-1 and 44 percent in the January 1997 Seattle Times, while filed 1996 quarters run $875k, $2,230k, $4,173k and $8,468k, roughly 17 to 19 percent per month within 1996, against FY1995 net sales of $511,000 in total. WHY THEY DIFFER: on repeat purchase, shifting definitions, a genuinely falling repeat share as acquisition accelerated, or an early flattering number; on growth, the soundbite may describe ORDERS rather than revenue, or a later window, or be compounded off an undisclosed base, and eighteen months back from January 1997 reaches January 1995, six months before the company says it sold anything. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: all figures are company-supplied and only the S-1 carries filing accountability, the FY1997 10-K later RAISING the repeat figure to "over 58%" and so confirming that the metric moved with its definition, while the S-1 itself states that "Growth rates experienced to date are not sustainable." BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: repeat purchase was real and material and growth was extraordinary, but no level is comparable across 1996 and 1997; cite the filing, log the 60 percent and the "34-35% a month for N months" as founder-supplied claims with undisclosed base, window and definition, and use filed quarters for magnitude. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: whether "sales" meant orders received or revenue booked is nowhere defined, and no 1995 figure of either kind exists. CONFIDENCE: High on the conflict, Low on any level. [src: D-30; D-25; D-26; J-14; C-66]
U.13 Claim: WHY SEATTLE — AND WHETHER THERE WAS A PLAN AT ALL. CLAIM A: Bezos in 1999, "The two primary factors… I wanted to be near a major book warehouse and near a pool of technical talent. Seattle had both." CLAIM B: Stone in 2013, the driver was sales-tax nexus, Bezos dropping Santa Cruz after learning of Quill Corp. v. North Dakota, 504 U.S. 298 (1992). CLAIM C: the 1997 LA Times, "Sales taxes dictated a small state. California and its 28 million book buyers were out", with Seattle chosen for high-tech workers and for being six hours from Roseburg, OREGON, "a major book-distribution center", and Kaphan's three-factor version independently naming sales tax, distribution proximity and talent, with the shortlist "Seattle or some unspecified location in Nevada". CLAIM D: the same 1997 article states that at the moment of the drive he "had no idea where he was going to live or how he would fund his scheme", the moving van's destination phoned in the next day. WHY THEY DIFFER: A, B and C are retrospective accounts optimising a story for different audiences; C is internally tense because its own distribution factor lands in OREGON, not Seattle; and D is a claim of no plan at all, flatly inconsistent with A's two-factor rationale. EVIDENCE WEIGHT: none is Tier 1 for 1994; the S-1 supplies the only primary corroboration that tax geography was REAL, Amazon collecting no sales or similar tax on shipments into any state other than Washington, but says nothing about why the office was where it was. BEST-SUPPORTED INTERPRETATION: treat the site decision as multi-causal and unproven in its weighting, the tax point being corroborated as a CONDITION and the talent-and-warehouse point only as a CLAIM, and treat the deliberate-strategist and no-plan-at-all tellings as two founder-authored versions of the same winter rather than as a resolved question. RESIDUAL UNCERTAINTY: high — no 1994 document in this corpus discusses location, and Nevada appears in no secondary account. CONFIDENCE: Medium on multi-causality, High on the absence of a contemporaneous rationale. [src: E-60; E-58; B-74; B-73; A-29; A-30; G-34]
